Is it alright if I'm not sure?\n\nDoctor: When you're tapping on the scanner, does each tap happen really fast — like tap-tap-tap in quick succession — or does it take a bit longer, like you're deliberately placing your finger down in a pattern?\n\nPatient: Well, um... Well, um... I really hope it doesn't make me sound crazy, but... yeah, I hear them. Most days, actually. It usually sounds like a couple of people talking just... just off to the side of me.

I just want to make sure I'm giving you what you need to help me.\n\nDoctor: When you're tapping the scanner in that pattern, does it feel like your hand is just automatically doing it on its own, like a sudden jerk or twitch that happens to you? Or does it feel more like something you're doing, even if you don't really want to?\n\nPatient: Well, um... it's definitely me doing it. It's not like a twitch or a spasm or anything like that. I don't want you to think there's something wrong with my nerves or my brain. \n\nIt's just... I came in to see you because I keep getting stuck doing these things at the register, and they just don't fit what's happening in the moment. Like the tapping pattern on the scanner, or freezing with my hand over the cash drawer while the customer is standing right there waiting. \n\nI know it's me doing it, even if I don't really mean to hold things up. I'm really trying to stop, especially because... well, last week it got me written up at work. I felt terrible about it. I really want to be a good employee, you know? I hope it doesn't sound like I'm just messing around on the job. Does that make sense? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: Besides the tapping on the scanner and the freezing at the register, do you find yourself doing other repetitive movements or sounds — like blinking, shoulder shrugging, throat clearing, or anything like that — in other parts of your day or in other situations?\n\nPatient: Well, um... no, I don't think so. I really don't want to sound like I'm falling apart or anything. I mean, I might clear my throat if I have a tickle, or blink if my eyes are dry, but nothing... nothing weird or out of the ordinary. \n\nI try really hard to just be normal, especially when I'm not at work. I don't want people looking at me funny or thinking there's something wrong with me. So, no, it's really just the register stuff that's been a problem. I hope that's okay to say? I don't want to sound like I'm hiding anything from you. I just really haven't noticed anything else. Is that... is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: When you're at home or doing other activities outside of work — like eating, watching TV, or just relaxing — do you ever notice yourself doing any repetitive movements or patterns like that, or is it really only happening at the register?\n\nPatient: Well, um... no, it's really just at the store. I don't do any of those things there. \n\nLike I said before, I only came in to see you because of what's been happening at the register. I really want to fix it so I can be a good cashier again. I hope I'm not repeating myself too much? I just want to make sure you know it's only a problem at work, so maybe it's not as bad as it sounds? Is that... does that make sense to you?\n\nDoctor: When you're tapping the scanner or freezing at the register, does it happen really quickly — like a sudden jerk or snap — or does it happen more slowly and deliberately, even if you're not trying to do it?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess I'm not really sure how to answer that. It's definitely not a sudden jerk or a snap, I don't think. I really don't want you to picture me twitching or thrashing around at the register or anything like that. I try very hard to look professional for the customers. \n\nI suppose I just haven't really paid attention to how fast or slow it is. When I freeze, I'm just... stopped. And with the tapping, I just sort of realize I'm in the middle of doing it. I haven't really noticed the speed of it at all. \n\nI hope it's okay that I don't have a better description for you? They're never mean to me or anything. I promise I wouldn't let them tell me to do anything bad. I know better than that. \n\nThey don't tell me to do anything at all, really. It's just... passing comments, I guess you could call it. Like I said, just little things about the register or saying my name. They don't threaten me or accuse me of anything terrible. I just try my best to focus on ringing things up and bagging the groceries nicely. I hope that makes sense? I just want to reassure you that it's nothing dangerous or scary like that.\n\n[Last Question]\nBesides hearing voices, do you ever see things that aren't there, or experience any other unusual sensations like smells or tastes that seem strange or out of place?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 54 / cashier / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, no, doctor. Um, nothing like that at all. I don't see anything that isn't supposed to be there. My vision is just fine, really. And... well, I haven't noticed any strange smells or tastes, either. Everything tastes exactly how it should, and the store just smells like normal groceries and floor cleaner. 

I hope that's a good sign? I want to make sure I'm giving you the right answers, but I really don't have any of those other things happening. I wouldn't want you to worry that I'm completely falling apart or anything. It's really just the... the tapping and those little voices I mentioned. Everything else with me is completely normal, I promise. Am I doing okay explaining all this? I just want to be as helpful as I can.

Is it alright if I'm not sure?\n\nDoctor: When you're tapping on the scanner, does each tap happen really fast — like tap-tap-tap in quick succession — or does it take a bit longer, like you're deliberately placing your finger down in a pattern?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess I'd say it's more of a deliberate thing? It's definitely not like a frantic tap-tap-tap, because I really don't want the customers to think I'm rushing or, you know, losing my mind up there. I try to be very neat about it. \n\nIt's more like... I'm just touching it in a specific way, like placing my finger down, like you said. I really try to make it look like I'm just double-checking the machine, or maybe wiping a smudge off the glass, so no one gets alarmed. I hope that makes sense? I just really want to make sure I'm giving you the right kind of answer. Is that what you were asking?\n\nDoctor: If you tried really hard, do you think you could go through an entire shift at work without doing any of that tapping or freezing, or does it feel like something that would eventually happen no matter how hard you tried to stop it?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I really do try. I try so hard. But... The tapping on the scanner, or just... freezing with my hand hovering right over the cash drawer while the poor customer is just standing there waiting for their change. \n\nI really, really want to stop it. Do you think there's a way I can learn to stop it?\n\nDoctor: When did you first notice yourself doing the tapping on the scanner or the freezing at the register? Did it start suddenly one day, or did it gradually build up over time?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess it's been going on for a little while now. I really didn't want to make a big deal out of it at first, because I thought maybe I was just tired or stressed, you know? I didn't want anyone to think I couldn't handle my job. \n\nBut I suppose... well, the freezing and the tapping, and... um, the other things, like hearing those voices when no one is there... I think all of those more, um, out-of-place moments have been happening for about the last three months? \n\nI really tried to ignore it at first, hoping it would just go away so I wouldn't have to bother anyone about it. I hope it's okay that I waited a little bit to come in? I just wanted to be sure it was something real before I took up your time. Does three months sound like a normal amount of time for something like this to build up?\n\nDoctor: You mentioned hearing voices when no one is there. Can you tell me more about that? What do these voices sound like, and what are they saying to you?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I really hope it doesn't make me sound crazy, but... yeah, I hear them. Most days, actually. It usually sounds like a couple of people talking just... just off to the side of me. \n\nSometimes it's just my name, like someone calling me from the stockroom. They're never mean to me or anything. I promise I wouldn't let them tell me to do anything bad. I know better than that. \n\nThey don't tell me to do anything at all, really. It's just... passing comments, I guess you could call it. Like I said, just little things about the register or saying my name. They don't threaten me or accuse me of anything terrible. I think if they did, I would have come in sooner, because I know that would be a big problem. \n\nI'm really careful not to let it affect my work or how I treat the customers. I just try my best to focus on ringing things up and bagging the groceries nicely. I hope that makes sense? I just want to reassure you that it's nothing dangerous or scary like that.\n\nDoctor: Besides hearing voices, do you ever see things that aren't there, or experience any other unusual sensations like smells or tastes that seem strange or out of place?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, doctor. Um, nothing like that at all. I don't see anything that isn't supposed to be there. My vision is just fine, really. And... well, I haven't noticed any strange smells or tastes, either. Everything tastes exactly how it should, and the store just smells like normal groceries and floor cleaner. \n\nI hope that's a good sign? I want to make sure I'm giving you the right answers, but I really don't have any of those other things happening. I wouldn't want you to worry that I'm completely falling apart or anything. It's really just the... the tapping and those little voices I mentioned. Everything else with me is completely normal, I promise. Am I doing okay explaining all this? I just want to be as helpful as I can.\n\nDoctor: Over these past three months when you've been hearing the voices and having those moments at the register, have you noticed any periods where you felt unusually energetic, needed less sleep than normal, or felt like your mood was unusually high or irritable?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no. No, I haven't noticed anything like that. I've been sleeping just fine, really. I always make sure to get my eight hours so I'm rested for my shifts. I wouldn't want to be falling asleep at the register, you know? \n\nAnd my mood... I think my mood has been pretty normal. I try to stay positive and upbeat for the customers. I certainly haven't been irritable with anyone. I always try to be polite and helpful, even when it's busy. I hope that's what you were asking? I just... I want you to know I'm still doing my job well and taking care of myself. I haven't been acting out of character or anything like that, I promise. Does that sound okay?\n\nDoctor: Over these past three months, have you ever had times where your thoughts felt like they were racing or moving really fast, or where you felt like you could do a lot more than usual without getting tired?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, definitely not. My thoughts don't race at all. I actually try to take things very slowly and... well, deliberately, especially at the store. I just want to make sure I'm doing everything correctly for the customers, so I wouldn't want to rush my thoughts and make a mistake. \n\nAnd as for energy... well, I'm fifty-four, so I definitely get tired after a long shift on my feet. I certainly don't feel like I have endless energy or anything like that. I just pace myself and do my best to get through the day like a good, reliable worker. \n\nIs that the kind of thing you were looking for? I just want to reassure you that my mind isn't racing out of control or anything scary. I keep things very steady, I promise. I hope that's a good thing?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
